A: Show answer
Human oversight is meaningful only when the assigned person understands the system's purpose and limitations, has access to relevant information, has sufficient time and competence to assess its output, and can intervene or override it without unreasonable penalty. A nominal reviewer who must approve hundreds of recommendations in seconds is not an effective safeguard. Oversight should be proportionate to the severity and reversibility of potential harm and should address automation bias, where people over-trust machine outputs. Article 14 of the EU AI Act requires high-risk systems to be designed for effective human oversight; UNESCO also emphasizes that ultimate responsibility remains attributable to people or legal entities.
Study instruction:
- Assess a human-review workflow for authority, competence, time, evidence access, override power, documentation, and protection from retaliation.
Key point:
- Human presence is not the same as meaningful human control.
- Reviewers need competence, independence, and real authority to intervene.
Source links:
- EU AI Act, Article 14: Human Oversight
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
41. Q: What is a fundamental-rights impact assessment, and why is it relevant to government AI?
A: Show answer
A fundamental-rights impact assessment examines how a proposed AI system may affect people and groups before deployment. It should describe the intended use, affected populations, data sources, decision pathways, likely harms, human oversight, and measures to prevent or mitigate those harms. It should consider unequal effects, privacy, expression, access to services, due process, and the ability to challenge outcomes. Article 27 of the EU AI Act requires such an assessment for specified deployers and high-risk uses within its scope; it is not a universal rule for every AI system in every jurisdiction. The assessment should be updated when material changes alter the risk.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short impact assessment for an AI system used in policing, welfare, or public employment; identify affected groups and realistic remedies.
Key point:
- Rights risks should be assessed before deployment, not only after harm occurs.
- Legal obligations depend on the system, deployer, use, and jurisdiction.
Source links:
- EU AI Act, Article 27: Fundamental Rights Impact Assessment
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
42. Q: How can AI-enabled surveillance interfere with privacy and other human rights?
A: Show answer
AI can increase the scale and speed of profiling, biometric identification, behavioural prediction, and automated decisions. The OHCHR's report on the right to privacy in the digital age explains that remote biometric recognition can enable authorities to identify and track people in public spaces, with implications for privacy, expression, peaceful assembly, association, and movement. Predictive systems may also reproduce historic discrimination in data and magnify the effect of small error rates when used at scale. A rights assessment must therefore examine necessity, proportionality, legal basis, accuracy, bias, retention, access, and remedy, not only whether the technology can perform the task.
Study instruction:
- Take a facial-recognition or predictive-policing example and identify affected rights, likely error pathways, and less intrusive alternatives.
Key point:
- Surveillance can chill lawful activity even without an arrest.
- Scaled prediction can magnify errors and discrimination.
Source links:
- OHCHR: The Right to Privacy in the Digital Age
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
43. Q: Why is freedom of expression especially important when governments use AI to manage information?
A: Show answer
Freedom of expression enables citizens, journalists, researchers, and public officials to question decisions and expose errors. AI can affect expression through content ranking, automated moderation, surveillance, synthetic propaganda, and the targeting of critics. Freedom House's 2025 report documents manipulation campaigns that include automated accounts and AI-generated misleading content, alongside censorship and surveillance. V-Dem's 2026 report identifies freedom of expression as one of the most frequently declining aspects of democracy. These findings do not establish that AI alone causes democratic decline; they show why AI deployment should be evaluated within the wider information environment and protected by independent media and legal safeguards.
Study instruction:
- Analyse one online manipulation campaign and distinguish censorship, coordinated amplification, synthetic content, and ordinary political persuasion.
Key point:
- Information integrity and freedom of expression must be protected together.
- AI can scale manipulation as well as legitimate communication.
Source links:
- Freedom on the Net 2025
- V-Dem Democracy Report 2026
44. Q: What data-governance safeguards are important when a state develops or procures AI?
A: Show answer
Safeguards should cover the full data lifecycle: lawful collection, purpose limitation, minimization, accuracy, representativeness, access control, retention, sharing, deletion, and secure storage. The agency should document data provenance, known gaps, sensitive attributes, and the consequences of linking separate datasets. Procurement should specify permitted uses, audit access, incident reporting, subcontractors, model updates, and exit arrangements. Privacy and human-rights impact assessments should occur before sensitive data are combined. The OHCHR and UNESCO guidance emphasizes safeguards throughout the AI lifecycle, while NIST provides a practical risk-management structure. These frameworks support governance; they do not replace applicable law.
Study instruction:
- Draft a data-governance checklist for a public AI procurement and assign responsibility for each lifecycle stage.
Key point:
- Data governance is a continuous control, not a one-time consent form.
- Procurement contracts must preserve audit, security, and exit rights.
Source links:
- OHCHR: The Right to Privacy in the Digital Age
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
45. Q: What should officials do if an AI system reports that a defence minister is planning a coup?
A: Show answer
Officials should treat the output as an allegation requiring urgent but independent verification, not as an order. They should preserve the model version, prompts, data sources, timestamps, confidence information, and audit logs; seek corroboration from sources not controlled by the same system; test for deepfakes, data poisoning, and alternative explanations; and involve lawful oversight and security procedures. Any coercive action must follow applicable law and due process. The system should not be allowed to conceal evidence, block independent review, or define the only acceptable response. If immediate danger is credible, emergency action should still be proportionate, documented, and reviewable.
Study instruction:
- Run a tabletop exercise with false-positive and genuine-threat branches; specify who verifies, who authorizes, what evidence is retained, and how decisions are reviewed.
Key point:
- High-stakes AI alerts require independent corroboration and lawful human authorization.
- Preserve evidence and reviewability even under time pressure.
Source links:
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
- EU AI Act, Article 14: Human Oversight
46. Q: How should a government respond if its chatbot points out a contradiction between constitutional guarantees and actual practice?
A: Show answer
The response should begin by checking the sources and determining whether the chatbot has accurately described the law and the relevant conduct. If the criticism is factually supported, suppressing the output does not resolve the underlying contradiction. Authorities should correct factual errors transparently, explain lawful limits where they exist, and preserve channels for public criticism and independent review. Developers should document content rules and the sources used by the system. A government should not treat an accurate, peaceful criticism as proof of disloyalty. The chapter's example highlights how a system that compares official statements with observable practice can expose contradictions, but model outputs still require verification.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a source-checking protocol that distinguishes accurate criticism, factual error, unlawful content, and disputed interpretation.
Key point:
- Verified criticism should prompt explanation and accountability, not automatic suppression.
- A chatbot's claim must be checked, but its political inconvenience is not evidence of error.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- Freedom on the Net 2025
47. Q: What safeguards are needed if a government uses AI to assign social-credit or risk scores to individuals?
A: Show answer
A scoring system that affects access to employment, benefits, education, travel, policing, or public services can create severe risks of discrimination, opaque punishment, and inability to contest a decision. Authorities should define a lawful and narrow purpose, assess necessity and proportionality, test data quality and group impacts, disclose meaningful reasons, provide human review and appeal, and prohibit unrelated reuse. Scores should not be treated as objective facts about a person's character. In the European Union, Article 5 of the AI Act prohibits specified social-scoring practices within its scope; that rule should not be generalized into a worldwide ban on every form of scoring.
Study instruction:
- Map the consequences of a hypothetical score and identify which decisions require notice, explanation, human review, correction, and independent appeal.
Key point:
- A score can become a powerful instrument of exclusion when linked to essential services.
- Legal prohibitions and obligations vary by jurisdiction and system design.
Source links:
- EU AI Act, Article 5: Prohibited AI Practices
- EU AI Act, Article 27: Fundamental Rights Impact Assessment
48. Q: What role should independent media, civil society, and public participation play in AI governance?
A: Show answer
Independent media and civil society provide information channels that can reveal failures, investigate procurement, represent affected groups, and challenge official narratives. Public participation can identify harms that technical developers overlook, especially where systems affect marginalized communities. These actors need access to relevant information, protection from retaliation, and meaningful opportunities to influence decisions. Participation should not be reduced to a consultation after the system has already been procured. UNESCO recommends inclusive governance and public awareness, while OECD principles emphasize human rights, democratic values, and accountability. Their effectiveness depends on institutional freedom and the ability to obtain remedies.
Study instruction:
- Design a public consultation for a high-impact government AI system, specifying when it occurs, who is represented, what information is disclosed, and how feedback changes the design.
Key point:
- Public oversight supplies knowledge and legitimacy that technical audits alone cannot provide.
- Participation requires transparency, protection, and a route to influence outcomes.
Source links:
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
- OECD AI Principles
49. Q: Why does the chapter compare international AI cooperation with efforts to control nuclear risks?
A: Show answer
The comparison emphasizes that technologies with cross-border consequences cannot be governed by one country acting alone. The Russell-Einstein Manifesto of 9 July 1955 called on people and leaders across political divisions to confront the shared danger of nuclear war. The chapter uses this as an analogy for AI: states with different political systems may still share an interest in avoiding catastrophic misuse or loss of control. The analogy has limits. AI is more widely distributed across civilian and commercial settings than nuclear weapons, and its risks vary greatly by application. Cooperation therefore needs both broad principles and practical mechanisms for testing, incident reporting, and accountability.
Study instruction:
- Compare nuclear-risk cooperation with AI governance in terms of verification, dual-use research, commercial actors, deployment speed, and enforcement.
Key point:
- Shared technological risks can create common interests across political divisions.
- AI cooperation must account for its diverse civilian and commercial uses.
Source links:
- Pugwash: The Russell-Einstein Manifesto
- OECD AI Principles
50. Q: What are the most important conclusions to draw from the chapter's argument about totalitarianism and algorithms?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that AI may alter the historical balance between centralized and distributed political systems by making large-scale information processing and surveillance more feasible. It also identifies a paradox: the same systems that help rulers monitor populations may become difficult for rulers to understand, supervise, or correct. The strongest lesson is institutional rather than deterministic. Concentrated information creates dependence; dependence creates opportunities for gatekeepers; and weak oversight makes errors and manipulation harder to detect. Yet AI can also support public services, independent analysis, and access to information. Outcomes depend on rights, contestability, plural sources, accountable human decisions, and international cooperation. The chapter explicitly presents algorithmic takeover as a possibility, not a prophecy.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter using four headings: information concentration, surveillance and censorship, algorithmic dependence, and institutional correction; give one safeguard for each.
Key point:
- AI can amplify existing political structures but does not determine them.
- The protection against concentrated algorithmic power is independent oversight, plural information, rights, and effective remedies.
Source links:
- V-Dem Democracy Report 2026
- NIST AI Risk Management Framework 1.0
- UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI
Rows: 50
Table 11
The Silicon Curtain: AI, Data Power, and Global Order
1. Q: What is the central argument of “The Silicon Curtain: Global Empire or Global Split?”
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that AI is not merely a technology that individual societies must govern internally. Because data, digital infrastructure, algorithms, markets, security systems and information flows cross borders, AI can reshape the distribution of power among societies. Harari identifies two broad geopolitical possibilities: a new concentration of authority in one or a few digital empires, or a fragmented world divided by rival digital networks. Either outcome could weaken national autonomy and collective capacity to manage global threats. The chapter’s deeper claim is that the decisive variable is human political choice: technology creates new capabilities, but institutions, incentives and leaders determine how those capabilities are used.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter’s two geopolitical pathways and explain why both depend on choices made by governments, corporations and societies.
Key point:
- AI is a global power and governance problem, not only a domestic technology issue.
- Human cooperation or rivalry shapes whether AI concentrates or fragments power.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
2. Q: Why does the chapter describe AI as a global problem rather than a problem confined to individual countries?
A: Show answer
Digital systems create cross-border externalities. A model trained in one jurisdiction can influence decisions, markets, political debate or security in another; data can be transferred across borders; cyber operations can disrupt foreign infrastructure; and a biological or information threat can spread internationally. A country may regulate its own developers responsibly yet remain exposed to systems, actors or infrastructure beyond its jurisdiction. This resembles climate change and infectious disease: national measures are necessary, but they cannot fully contain risks whose causes and consequences cross borders. Effective governance therefore requires domestic safeguards combined with international coordination, information sharing and compatible standards.
Study instruction:
- Map one AI-related risk across at least three countries and identify which parts can be controlled nationally and which require cooperation.
Key point:
- Cross-border effects make purely national regulation insufficient.
- International coordination complements rather than replaces domestic safeguards.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- WHO: Sharing International Health Regulations Information
3. Q: Why does the chapter locate the existential danger of AI primarily in human shortcomings rather than in machine malevolence?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s distinction is between a system independently choosing to destroy humanity and people deploying fallible or poorly controlled systems in a divided, competitive world. A dictator may delegate consequential authority to an unreliable model; a terrorist may use automated tools for destructive ends; rival states may rush deployment because they fear falling behind; and corporations may externalize harms to preserve market share. In each case, the danger emerges from human decisions, institutional weakness, misaligned incentives and inadequate oversight. This does not imply that technical failures are harmless. It means that technical risk becomes catastrophic through the social and political arrangements that give systems authority and access to consequential capabilities.
Study instruction:
- For three AI failure scenarios, separate the technical failure, the human decision that enabled it, and the institutional control that could have interrupted the chain.
Key point:
- Catastrophic outcomes can arise without a malicious machine.
- Governance must address human incentives as well as technical reliability.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
4. Q: How could AI increase the danger of nuclear escalation or accidental nuclear use?
A: Show answer
AI could influence intelligence analysis, early-warning interpretation, target assessment, command-and-control communications or the speed at which leaders receive recommendations. If decision-makers treat an opaque model’s output as authoritative, a false alert or misclassified event could be given undue weight. Automation may also compress the time available to verify information, consult advisers and communicate with an adversary. A further risk is that conventional AI-enabled systems could threaten assets connected to nuclear forces, creating uncertainty about whether an opponent is preparing a disarming attack. The chapter’s warning is not that an AI can simply launch a nuclear weapon by itself in every system; the risk depends on the authority delegated, the architecture, safeguards and human decision procedures.
Study instruction:
- Draw a nuclear-crisis decision chain and mark where false data, model uncertainty, cyber compromise or time pressure could distort human judgment.
Key point:
- AI can raise nuclear risk even when it is not directly controlling a nuclear warhead.
- Human verification, resilient communications and decision time are critical safeguards.
Source links:
- SIPRI: Impact of Military AI on Nuclear Escalation Risk
- SIPRI: Artificial Intelligence, Strategic Stability and Nuclear Risk
5. Q: How could AI amplify biological threats, and what safeguards are relevant?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses a deliberately extreme scenario in which AI lowers barriers to harmful biological research, procurement or dissemination planning. The policy lesson is about dual-use capability: tools and knowledge developed for legitimate science can also be misused, while digital systems can accelerate search, coordination and decision-making. This should not be read as evidence that a model can autonomously create and release a pandemic pathogen today. Risk reduction requires biosafety and biosecurity oversight, screening and accountability in relevant supply chains, responsible research practices, rapid public-health surveillance, and international notification when a cross-border health threat emerges. Controls should focus on preventing misuse without unnecessarily obstructing beneficial medical research.
Study instruction:
- Identify the governance stages in a hypothetical biological-risk incident, from research oversight to public-health detection and international notification, without detailing pathogen construction or release methods.
Key point:
- AI can amplify dual-use risks, but technical capability and real-world execution must not be conflated.
- Biosafety, biosecurity and public-health cooperation are complementary layers.
Source links:
- WHO: Sharing International Health Regulations Information
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
6. Q: What does the chapter mean by “weapons of social mass destruction”?
A: Show answer
The phrase describes information operations that damage the social conditions required for collective life. Synthetic media, fabricated identities, coordinated falsehoods, impersonation and fraudulent digital assets can make people doubt the authenticity of messages, institutions, evidence and one another. The harm is not limited to persuading someone of one false claim. Persistent manipulation can weaken trust, intensify polarization, discredit legitimate reporting and make coordinated responses to emergencies more difficult. The phrase is an analytical metaphor, not a formal legal category. Its usefulness lies in highlighting that social trust and shared information are critical infrastructure even though they are not physical structures.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a disinformation case by identifying the target audience, the trust relationship being attacked, the likely social consequence and a proportionate response.
Key point:
- Information manipulation can weaken the trust on which institutions depend.
- Resilience requires transparency, media literacy, credible institutions and accountability.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact — Information Integrity
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
7. Q: How do cross-border externalities change the logic of AI regulation?
A: Show answer
A regulator must consider not only harms within its territory but also the effects of domestic firms, exported systems, outsourced data processing and internationally connected infrastructure. Conversely, it must consider risks imported through foreign platforms, models, cloud services and supply chains. A purely territorial approach can leave gaps when responsibilities are divided among developers, deployers, cloud providers and users in different jurisdictions. International cooperation can reduce those gaps through shared incident reporting, compatible risk classifications, minimum safeguards and channels for technical consultation. Harmonization need not mean identical national laws; it can mean that different systems recognize comparable protections and can cooperate when incidents cross borders.
Study instruction:
- Build a responsibility map for a hypothetical AI service developed in one country, hosted in another and used by a public agency in a third.
Key point:
- Cross-border AI services distribute both benefits and accountability.
- Interoperable safeguards can close gaps without requiring identical laws.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- OECD: AI Principles
8. Q: What does the chapter mean by a “postimperial era,” and why do small states matter in its analysis?
A: Show answer
Harari uses the term to describe an international order in which many former colonies possess formal sovereignty and some ability to bargain among larger powers, rather than being directly governed by a few empires. Small states can use strategic location, diplomatic alignment, resources, votes in international bodies or competition among investors to secure concessions. This does not mean that power is equal or that economic dependence has disappeared. The point is that sovereignty and leverage are distributed more widely than in classical territorial empires. The chapter asks whether digital infrastructure and AI could reverse some of that dispersion by making smaller states dependent on foreign platforms, data systems and computing resources.
Study instruction:
- Choose a small state and identify three sources of bargaining leverage and three forms of digital dependence that could reduce its autonomy.
Key point:
- Formal independence does not guarantee substantive technological autonomy.
- Digital dependence can alter the bargaining power of smaller states.
Source links:
- United Nations Charter: Purposes and Principles
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
9. Q: What is a digital empire in the context of the chapter?
A: Show answer
A digital empire is a system of influence in which a state or corporate-state coalition gains disproportionate control over other societies through data, computing infrastructure, platforms, algorithms, technical standards and access to essential digital services. Its power need not involve formal annexation or direct administration. It can operate through dependency: local institutions may rely on foreign cloud services, payment networks, identity systems, AI models or communications platforms that they cannot readily replace or govern. The term is a political analogy rather than a settled legal category. To assess whether a relationship is imperial in effect, examine who owns the infrastructure, who sets the rules, who captures value, who can deny access and whether the dependent party has realistic alternatives.
Study instruction:
- Apply the five questions of ownership, rule-setting, value capture, access control and alternatives to one foreign digital service used by a public institution.
Key point:
- Digital dominance can operate through dependency without territorial conquest.
- The distribution of control and value is more revealing than the label attached to a technology.
Source links:
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
- Couldry and Mejias: Data Colonialism
10. Q: What is the historical analogy between the Industrial Revolution and the AI race?
A: Show answer
The chapter compares the early commercial development of railways and steam technology with the initial growth of modern AI in private companies. In both cases, entrepreneurs developed capabilities before many governments fully understood their strategic significance. Once states recognized the economic and military consequences, technology became part of national competition and imperial strategy. The analogy illustrates how a commercial innovation can become geopolitical infrastructure. It is not a claim that AI will reproduce nineteenth-century imperialism in exactly the same way: data, software, network effects and global supply chains differ fundamentally from coal, railways and industrial machinery. Historical analogies should identify mechanisms, not substitute for analysis of present conditions.
Study instruction:
- Compare one industrial-era technology and one AI-era capability using the same categories: early innovators, state adoption, strategic uses, dependencies and distribution of gains.
Key point:
- Technologies can move from commercial tools to instruments of state power.
- Historical parallels are useful only when their limits are also stated.
Source links:
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
11. Q: Why did private corporations become early leaders in the AI race before governments fully mobilized?
A: Show answer
Private technology companies had commercial incentives to gather large datasets, build computing infrastructure, attract specialized researchers and deploy products at scale. Search, advertising, e-commerce, social networking and cloud services generated both revenue and data that could improve machine-learning systems. Governments initially relied on these firms for innovation and infrastructure, but later recognized the strategic implications for economic competitiveness, surveillance, defense and national security. The resulting contest is therefore not simply state versus state: it often involves partnerships, procurement, regulation, investment and tension between governments and companies. Private ownership can accelerate innovation while creating accountability and dependency problems when essential infrastructure is concentrated.
Study instruction:
- Map the relationship among a government, a cloud provider, a model developer and a public-sector user; identify each party’s incentives and obligations.
Key point:
- Commercial incentives helped build capabilities that later acquired strategic importance.
- Public authority and private infrastructure are increasingly interdependent.
Source links:
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
12. Q: Why does the chapter highlight AlexNet and AlphaGo as turning points in the AI race?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses AlexNet’s 2012 ImageNet result and AlphaGo’s 2016 public victory over a leading Go player as visible signals that machine-learning methods had crossed important performance thresholds. AlexNet demonstrated the power of deep convolutional networks for image recognition at scale; AlphaGo demonstrated that neural networks combined with search and reinforcement learning could master a complex strategic game. The geopolitical importance was not that either system directly created a military capability. Rather, the events made the pace and strategic potential of AI more legible to executives, policymakers and the public. The chapter’s chronology is a historical interpretation: AI progress was cumulative and did not begin with one event.
Study instruction:
- Read the original AlexNet paper abstract and the Nature account of AlphaGo, then explain what each demonstrated and what it did not prove.
Key point:
- Breakthrough demonstrations can change political attention and investment.
- AI development is cumulative, not the result of a single eureka moment.
Source links:
- Krizhevsky, Sutskever and Hinton: ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks
- Nature: The Go Files — AI Computer Wins First Match Against Master Go Player
13. Q: Why is data a strategic resource for AI development?
A: Show answer
Data can help train, evaluate and adapt models to recognize patterns, predict outcomes and perform tasks. Its value depends on quality, representativeness, lawful access, context, labeling, infrastructure and the ability to convert it into useful systems. Large volumes alone do not guarantee a capable or fair model; biased, incomplete or poorly governed data can reproduce errors and exclusion. Data can also create strategic advantage when a firm or state controls unique datasets, user relationships or the infrastructure through which data flows. The chapter’s “raw material” analogy is useful, but data is not identical to a mineral: it can be copied, combined and reused, and its value is produced through institutions, skills and computation as well as collection.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a dataset for provenance, consent, representativeness, quality, permitted use, security and the local value created from it.
Key point:
- Data is an input to AI value creation, not value in itself.
- Governance and capabilities determine who benefits from data.
Source links:
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
14. Q: What is “data colonialism,” and how does it differ from classical territorial colonialism?
A: Show answer
Data colonialism is a scholarly concept describing the appropriation and monetization of data generated through everyday social life, often by actors that control the platforms and infrastructure through which people interact. The chapter extends the concept to international power: societies may supply behavioral, commercial, cultural or public-sector data while foreign firms capture much of the analytical capability, intellectual property, revenue and strategic influence. Unlike classical colonialism, the process may occur without direct occupation or formal political rule. The analogy is strongest when extraction is asymmetric and people or institutions have little control over collection, use or value distribution. It should not be used to label every cross-border data transfer as colonial exploitation.
Study instruction:
- Apply the concept to one platform and distinguish ordinary data exchange from extraction marked by unequal control, limited choice and unequal value capture.
Key point:
- Data colonialism concerns power relations around appropriation and value, not just data location.
- The colonial analogy is an analytical framework that must be tested against evidence.
Source links:
- Couldry and Mejias: Data Colonialism
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
15. Q: How could access to the personal data of a country’s political and economic elites create geopolitical leverage?
A: Show answer
Detailed personal information may reveal relationships, preferences, vulnerabilities, conflicts of interest, financial exposures or patterns of behavior. If a foreign actor obtains such information unlawfully or without meaningful safeguards, it could support intelligence collection, targeted manipulation, coercion, blackmail or strategic influence. The risk is not that every data holder will automatically exploit every record. It is that concentrated access creates capabilities that the data subjects and their institutions may not be able to inspect or contest. A responsible assessment should distinguish lawful public-interest information from intimate personal data and should consider necessity, proportionality, access controls, retention, redress and independent oversight.
Study instruction:
- Create a data-threat model for a senior public official and identify the minimum collection, access, retention and audit controls needed to reduce abuse.
Key point:
- Sensitive data can become an instrument of influence when control is asymmetric.
- Privacy protection is also a national-security and institutional-integrity issue.
Source links:
- United Nations: International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
16. Q: How can digital platforms exercise geopolitical influence through control of attention?
A: Show answer
Platforms shape which information is visible, how content is recommended, what becomes popular and which audiences are reached. Their ranking and recommendation systems can influence political agendas, social movements, consumer behavior and perceptions of public events. The effects depend on platform design, local context, user behavior, media institutions and political actors; an algorithm does not mechanically determine public opinion. Influence becomes especially concerning when opaque systems amplify inflammatory or deceptive material, enable covert targeting or provide unequal access to attention. Governance should therefore examine advertising transparency, recommender-system risks, political content, platform accountability, user choice and freedom of expression together.
Study instruction:
- Trace how one piece of political content moves from publication to recommendation, amplification and audience response; identify where transparency or accountability is needed.
Key point:
- Control over distribution can be as consequential as control over content creation.
- Platform accountability must protect both information integrity and fundamental rights.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact — Information Integrity
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
17. Q: Why do governments restrict foreign digital applications, and what trade-offs should they consider?
A: Show answer
Governments may cite national security, data access, foreign influence, cybersecurity, privacy, critical infrastructure or public-order concerns. Some restrictions may be justified where credible evidence shows serious risks that cannot be mitigated through less restrictive measures. However, broad bans can also reduce competition, limit expression and access to information, disrupt businesses and accelerate fragmentation of the global Internet. A sound policy should identify the specific threat, disclose evidence to the extent possible, apply proportionate and reviewable measures, provide due process and distinguish a provider’s nationality from demonstrated security risk. The chapter describes app bans as symptoms of a wider geopolitical split, but each measure requires case-specific evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Assess one app restriction using a written test of evidence, necessity, proportionality, alternatives, review and effects on users.
Key point:
- Digital sovereignty can protect legitimate interests but can also become a rationale for overbroad control.
- Risk-based safeguards are preferable to unsupported assumptions about nationality.
Source links:
- Internet Society: Internet Fragmentation Explainer
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
18. Q: How should claims about China’s social credit system be understood accurately?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses social credit as an example of data-driven governance, but the phrase should not be taken to mean that China operates one simple, universal numerical score for every citizen. Research describes a complex and evolving set of national and local mechanisms, regulatory records, blacklists, credit information systems and pilot programs, with different purposes and levels of implementation. Some local schemes have used individual scores, but this does not establish a single all-encompassing national rating. The important analytical questions are what data are collected, which agencies use them, what consequences follow, how errors are corrected and what legal protections exist. Similar questions should also be asked of private scoring and reputation systems elsewhere.
Study instruction:
- Compare a government credit-record mechanism, a financial credit score and a commercial platform rating; list their data, decision-makers, consequences and appeal routes.
Key point:
- Avoid treating the Chinese system as one uniform nationwide citizen score.
- Evaluate concrete mechanisms and their actual legal and social effects.
Source links:
- MERICS: China’s Social Credit System in 2021
- Liu and Rona-Tas: Trusting by Numbers
19. Q: How can data colonialism create an unequal international distribution of economic value?
A: Show answer
A society may generate valuable data through its consumers, workers, public services, language communities and local businesses, while foreign firms control the models, cloud infrastructure, intellectual property, distribution channels and customer relationships that monetize it. If local firms cannot build, adapt or negotiate access to those capabilities, they may receive limited returns and remain dependent on imported services. The inequality is not inevitable: data-sharing agreements, competition policy, public-interest data institutions, local research capacity, skills and regional markets can help distribute value more fairly. The relevant question is not simply where a server is located but who contributes, controls, learns, earns and bears the risks across the value chain.
Study instruction:
- Draw a value-chain diagram for a local dataset used by a foreign AI service and identify where value, bargaining power and risk accumulate.
Key point:
- Data extraction can reinforce existing global inequalities when capabilities and profits remain concentrated.
- Local value creation depends on skills, institutions, infrastructure and bargaining power.
Source links:
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
- Couldry and Mejias: Data Colonialism
20. Q: Why can information and algorithmic power be concentrated more easily than land or many industrial assets?
A: Show answer
Digital information can be transmitted across borders at very low marginal cost, while software and algorithms can be replicated and deployed across many locations without physically relocating factories or natural resources. Advanced compute, skilled labor, intellectual property, cloud platforms and network effects can therefore create powerful centers of control. Yet concentration is not unlimited: data may be legally restricted, compute depends on energy and hardware, models require maintenance and local adaptation, and governments can create alternatives or regulate access. The chapter’s contrast with land and industrial assets highlights the potential for centralization, but material infrastructure and political institutions still constrain it.
Study instruction:
- Compare a land-based industry, a factory-based industry and a data-intensive service by identifying which assets are geographically fixed and which can be centralized.
Key point:
- Digital assets are unusually scalable and transferable, enabling concentrated control.
- Compute, energy, hardware, law and local context still limit centralization.
Source links:
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
21. Q: How could AI automation change the economic position of countries dependent on labor-intensive manufacturing?
A: Show answer
If robotics, AI-enabled production planning and advanced manufacturing reduce the cost of producing goods closer to major consumer markets, countries that rely on low-cost labor may face pressure on exports and employment. The effects will vary by sector, adoption costs, infrastructure, trade access, labor skills and the ability to move into higher-value activities. Automation can displace tasks, transform jobs and create new occupations; it does not imply that all manufacturing will return to wealthy countries or that every worker will be replaced. The distributional challenge is that firms and countries with capital, compute and skills may capture gains more quickly than workers and regions with fewer resources to adapt.
Study instruction:
- For a labor-intensive export sector, identify automatable tasks, vulnerable worker groups, possible new roles and the training or industrial policies required for transition.
Key point:
- Automation can redistribute production and bargaining power across countries.
- The speed and fairness of transition depend on investment, education and social protection.
Source links:
- ILO: Generative AI and Jobs — 2025 Global Index
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
22. Q: What foundations do developing countries need to participate meaningfully in the AI economy?
A: Show answer
The World Bank’s 2025 report organizes AI readiness around four foundations: connectivity, compute, context and competency. Connectivity includes reliable electricity, broadband and devices. Compute covers affordable access to chips, servers, data centers and cloud capacity. Context means relevant data, models and applications that reflect local languages, cultures and needs. Competency includes the skills and institutional capabilities required to adopt, adapt and innovate responsibly. These foundations are interdependent: connectivity without skills may produce limited gains, while local data without compute or governance may not translate into useful systems. Countries can pursue adoption first, then adaptation and innovation as capacity grows.
Study instruction:
- Score a country or organization against the four foundations and identify the two highest-priority gaps, with practical investments for each.
Key point:
- AI readiness is broader than owning a model or buying software.
- Infrastructure, compute, local context and skills must develop together.
Source links:
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
23. Q: What is the “Silicon Curtain”?
A: Show answer
The Silicon Curtain is the chapter’s metaphor for a geopolitical boundary formed by code, digital infrastructure, hardware, regulation, data flows and platform ecosystems rather than by barbed wire or a conventional border. A person’s digital environment can determine which services are available, which information is accessible, where personal data is stored, which standards apply and which institutions can exercise control. The metaphor emphasizes that digital divisions can be embedded in ordinary devices and services. It is not a single physical wall or a formally demarcated border; it describes the cumulative effect of policy and technical choices that make digital spheres less interoperable.
Study instruction:
- Inventory the software, hardware, cloud services, identity systems and data routes used by one organization, then identify dependencies that could place it within a particular digital sphere.
Key point:
- Digital borders can be built into everyday infrastructure and software.
- Fragmentation is produced by interacting technical, economic and political decisions.
Source links:
- Internet Society: Internet Fragmentation Explainer
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
24. Q: Why are rival digital spheres not simply local versions of the same technology?
A: Show answer
Digital systems reflect the incentives and institutions under which they are developed. Different jurisdictions may assign different roles to governments, private companies, security agencies and users; they may adopt different rules for privacy, speech, competition, surveillance and data access. These differences can influence product design, platform architecture, technical standards and the information environment. It is therefore misleading to assume that two services with similar labels are functionally identical. At the same time, broad comparisons between national systems can oversimplify internal diversity, regulatory change and variation among firms. The chapter’s contrast is best treated as a structural argument, not as a complete description of every user or institution in either country.
Study instruction:
- Compare two digital services by governance, business model, data practices, interoperability, user rights and relationship with public authorities rather than by brand name alone.
Key point:
- Technology embodies institutional and commercial choices.
- National digital models are internally diverse and should not be reduced to stereotypes.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- OECD: AI Principles
25. Q: How can hardware supply chains and export controls contribute to a Silicon Curtain?
A: Show answer
Advanced AI depends on semiconductors, fabrication equipment, networking, data centers, electricity and specialized software. Governments may restrict exports or investment when they believe certain components could strengthen a strategic rival’s military or technological capabilities. Such controls can slow access to critical inputs in the short term while encouraging the targeted country to develop alternative suppliers, standards and infrastructure over time. Fragmentation may increase costs, reduce interoperability and create competing ecosystems. Controls also create dependencies among allies and private suppliers. Their effectiveness should be assessed against stated security objectives, circumvention risks, economic costs and the possibility of accelerating technological separation.
Study instruction:
- Map the supply chain for one AI hardware component and identify concentration points, export-control exposure, substitute suppliers and resilience options.
Key point:
- Hardware and compute access are geopolitical resources.
- Export restrictions can both constrain rivals and accelerate parallel technology ecosystems.
Source links:
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
26. Q: What are the main risks of fragmenting the global Internet into separate digital spheres?
A: Show answer
Fragmentation can reduce interoperability, increase compliance and transaction costs, limit access to information, weaken competition and make cross-border research or emergency coordination more difficult. Separate technical standards and incompatible services can lock users and organizations into particular ecosystems. Political fragmentation can also produce distinct information environments in which groups lack shared sources or common procedures for verifying claims. However, not every national regulation or data-localization requirement fragments the Internet to the same degree; some measures can coexist with open interconnection. The critical test is whether a policy materially undermines global connectivity, interoperability and the ability to communicate across networks.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a digital policy by measuring its effects on connectivity, interoperability, information access, competition and cross-border collaboration.
Key point:
- Fragmentation has technical, economic, political and social consequences.
- Safeguards should protect legitimate public interests while preserving global interconnection where possible.
Source links:
- Internet Society: Internet Fragmentation Explainer
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
27. Q: How could separate digital “cocoons” weaken a shared human reality?
A: Show answer
When people rely on different platforms, recommendation systems, search tools, data sources and automated interpreters, they may encounter different facts, explanations and social priorities. Over time, these environments can reinforce distinct identities and make cross-group communication harder. The risk is not simply that people disagree; pluralism is compatible with cooperation. The deeper problem arises when groups cannot establish common evidence, understand each other’s terms or verify what the other side is seeing. A shared reality does not require uniform beliefs. It requires enough common procedures, trustworthy information and channels of communication to debate differences and coordinate action.
Study instruction:
- Compare how two information systems present the same public event and identify both factual divergences and differences in framing or evidence.
Key point:
- Information cocoons can make disagreement harder to resolve.
- Shared verification practices matter more than complete uniformity of opinion.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact — Information Integrity
- Internet Society: Internet Fragmentation Explainer
28. Q: What is the mind-body problem, and why does the chapter connect it to digital culture?
A: Show answer
The mind-body problem concerns the relationship between mental experience and the physical body: whether persons are fundamentally embodied organisms, minds associated with bodies, or something else. Harari uses historical religious and philosophical disagreements to illustrate how ideas about embodiment can shape identity, morality and social institutions. He then asks whether immersive digital environments may revive these questions in new forms. The historical examples are interpretive and should not be mistaken for a complete account of Christian theology or philosophy. Their role is to show that apparently abstract ideas about personhood can influence how societies value physical life, virtual experience and nonhuman entities.
Study instruction:
- Define at least two philosophical positions on embodiment and explain how each might interpret a person’s online identity differently.
Key point:
- Concepts of identity influence social and political judgments.
- Digital technology gives old philosophical questions new practical consequences.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
29. Q: How might digital identities challenge assumptions about the relationship between online and physical life?
A: Show answer
People increasingly work, learn, socialize, create, transact and maintain relationships through digital representations. These activities can be meaningful and consequential even though they occur through screens, avatars or mediated communication. The chapter contrasts a view that treats online life as secondary to embodied existence with a view that sees virtual identity as a space for expanded self-expression. In practice, the two are interdependent: online activity relies on bodies, devices, electricity, labor, law and physical infrastructure, while digital representations can affect offline opportunities and relationships. A useful analysis avoids treating virtual and physical life as mutually exclusive worlds.
Study instruction:
- List three online activities and trace their dependence on physical resources, legal identity, human relationships and offline consequences.
Key point:
- Digital life is meaningful but materially dependent.
- Online and offline identities influence one another.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- United Nations: International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights
30. Q: Why could a society’s view of embodiment affect its environmental and infrastructure priorities?
A: Show answer
If a society regards human identity and well-being as inseparable from physical bodies, it may place strong emphasis on clean air, water, sanitation, housing, health systems, food systems and functioning ecosystems. If it treats virtual experience as the primary arena of life, it may be more willing to underweight the material systems that sustain digital and biological existence. The chapter presents this as a possible cultural divergence, not a prediction that digital societies will necessarily neglect nature. In reality, digital services depend on energy, minerals, cooling, transport and waste management. A responsible digital strategy therefore evaluates environmental impacts across the full lifecycle of hardware, data centers and AI services.
Study instruction:
- Assess one AI service’s physical footprint, including energy, water, hardware, minerals, supply chains and end-of-life waste.
Key point:
- Digital systems remain dependent on ecological and material foundations.
- Environmental responsibility should be built into technology governance.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
31. Q: What is the difference between treating an AI as a useful tool and recognizing it as a legal or moral person?
A: Show answer
A tool can perform valuable or complex tasks without having legal rights or moral standing of its own. Legal personhood is a status created by law for specific purposes; corporations, for example, can hold rights and duties without being human beings. Moral personhood is a philosophical question about whether an entity has interests, consciousness, agency or other characteristics that warrant direct moral consideration. The chapter speculates that societies that downplay biological embodiment may become more open to recognizing AI entities as persons. That is not an established consequence of digitalization. Current policy must distinguish capability, apparent conversational behavior, consciousness claims, legal responsibility and the rights of human beings affected by AI systems.
Study instruction:
- Prepare separate definitions of technical capability, legal personhood and moral status, then test them against a hypothetical AI assistant.
Key point:
- Sophisticated behavior does not by itself establish consciousness or moral personhood.
- Legal status and moral status are distinct questions requiring careful reasoning.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- United Nations: International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights
32. Q: How could disagreements about AI rights and virtual identity become political or cultural conflicts?
A: Show answer
Societies may disagree about whether identity should be grounded primarily in biological embodiment, personal autonomy, social relationships, legal status or digital self-presentation. They may also disagree about whether advanced AI systems deserve protection, whether avatars can represent legally recognized persons, and who is accountable when a digital agent acts. If these disagreements become embedded in education, law, religion, employment or public services, they may affect diplomacy and cross-border recognition. Harari presents possible conflicts as speculative scenarios, not forecasts. A practical response is to establish rights-based principles, transparent legal categories and procedures for resolving disputes before incompatible assumptions become entrenched.
Study instruction:
- Draft a short policy comparison showing how two jurisdictions might regulate avatars, AI agents and responsibility for digital actions, while identifying common rights protections.
Key point:
- Identity debates can influence law and political belonging.
- The chapter’s future cultural conflicts are hypotheses, not settled predictions.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
33. Q: Why might the future digital order contain more than two competing empires?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that the United States and China are prominent AI powers but that other states and regional blocs may seek their own digital spheres. The number of centers could depend on technological capability, market size, political preferences, access to compute, regulatory capacity, language and regional cooperation. Multipolarity might reduce dependence on a single hegemon by offering alternatives, but it could also multiply incompatible standards and strategic rivalries. More centers do not automatically produce more freedom or stability. The key issue is whether these systems remain interoperable and whether states can cooperate on shared risks despite political competition.
Study instruction:
- Compare a bipolar and a multipolar digital order using criteria of concentration, interoperability, bargaining power, security and governance capacity.
Key point:
- Multipolarity can diversify power while increasing coordination challenges.
- The number of digital blocs does not determine whether the system is cooperative.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- OECD: AI Principles
34. Q: How does cyber warfare differ from nuclear warfare in its strategic characteristics?
A: Show answer
Cyber operations can be tailored to targets ranging from individual devices to critical infrastructure, may be reversible or persistent, and can remain covert for extended periods. Attribution is often difficult, effects may be uncertain and the threshold between espionage, disruption and armed attack can be contested. Nuclear weapons, by contrast, have highly visible and potentially catastrophic physical effects, creating a different deterrence environment. Cyber capabilities can therefore be used below the threshold of open war, but repeated operations can still trigger retaliation or escalation. The comparison does not mean nuclear deterrence is simple or cyber operations are harmless; it shows why crisis management and signaling are more ambiguous in cyberspace.
Study instruction:
- Compare a cyber incident and a nuclear attack across visibility, attribution, reversibility, effects, warning time and escalation pathways.
Key point:
- Cyber conflict creates ambiguity that complicates deterrence and response.
- Low-visibility operations can still have serious civilian and strategic consequences.
Source links:
- SIPRI: Artificial Intelligence, Strategic Stability and Nuclear Risk
- ICRC: International Humanitarian Law Limits the Conduct of Cyber Operations
35. Q: Why are attribution and uncertainty major problems in cyber conflict?
A: Show answer
A cyber incident may be routed through compromised infrastructure, intermediaries or criminal networks, making it difficult to identify the operator, sponsor or intended purpose. Technical indicators can be misleading, incomplete or deliberately manipulated. A state that attributes an incident too quickly may punish the wrong actor or escalate a crisis based on weak evidence. Conversely, excessive delay may encourage repeated attacks if perpetrators believe they will not be identified. A robust response combines technical forensics, intelligence, independent review, allied consultation, confidence levels and careful public communication. Attribution should be treated as an evidentiary judgment with degrees of confidence, not a binary guess made under political pressure.
Study instruction:
- Write an incident-attribution protocol that separates observed technical facts, analytic inference, confidence level, alternative explanations and the threshold for public accusation.
Key point:
- Attribution errors can turn a technical incident into a diplomatic or military crisis.
- Transparent confidence assessments reduce the risk of premature retaliation.
Source links:
- United Nations: Report on Responsible State Behaviour in Cyberspace (A/76/135)
- ICRC: Cyber Operations and Harmful Information
36. Q: How does uncertainty about cyber capabilities undermine traditional deterrence?
A: Show answer
Deterrence depends partly on beliefs about an opponent’s capabilities, intentions and likely response. In cyberspace, states may not know which systems have been compromised, whether malware will function under crisis conditions, or whether their own command networks can be trusted. This uncertainty can encourage worst-case planning, pre-emptive action or exaggerated claims of vulnerability. It can also create false confidence if leaders believe they possess an advantage that does not exist. Risk reduction therefore requires resilient systems, realistic testing, incident communication, shared crisis channels and limits on operations that could threaten essential civilian services or strategic stability.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a hypothetical cyber crisis from both sides and list the assumptions each government might make about hidden capabilities and retaliation.
Key point:
- Uncertainty can produce both overconfidence and excessive fear.
- Resilience and communication are essential complements to deterrence.
Source links:
- United Nations: Report on Responsible State Behaviour in Cyberspace (A/76/135)
- SIPRI: Artificial Intelligence, Strategic Stability and Nuclear Risk
37. Q: What is first-strike instability, and why could AI intensify it?
A: Show answer
First-strike instability arises when a state believes that acting first could prevent or sharply reduce the damage it would suffer from an opponent’s response. If leaders fear that their advantage is temporary, they may feel pressure to act before the window closes. AI could contribute by accelerating intelligence analysis, improving target detection, increasing the perceived vulnerability of command systems or creating uncertainty about an adversary’s defensive capabilities. These effects may be exaggerated or misunderstood, but perceptions can still influence decisions. Stability requires credible second-strike capabilities, secure communications, human deliberation and crisis arrangements that reduce incentives to act on incomplete information.
Study instruction:
- Construct a crisis timeline and identify the points at which perceived advantage, warning time or uncertainty could create pressure for pre-emption.
Key point:
- Perceived temporary advantage can create incentives for dangerous early action.
- AI-related uncertainty may destabilize decisions even when actual capabilities are limited.
Source links:
- SIPRI: Impact of Military AI on Nuclear Escalation Risk
- SIPRI: Artificial Intelligence, Strategic Stability and Nuclear Risk
38. Q: How can AI compress decision time during a military crisis?
A: Show answer
AI-enabled surveillance, sensor fusion, pattern recognition and decision-support systems can process information faster than human teams. Faster analysis may improve awareness, but it can also encourage leaders to shorten verification, consultation and diplomatic communication. If an automated system presents uncertain output as a precise prediction, decision-makers may feel compelled to respond before they understand the evidence. The danger is not speed alone; it is speed combined with high stakes, opaque reasoning, poor data, organizational pressure and weak authority controls. Crisis procedures should preserve time for human judgment, independent corroboration and communication with the opposing side.
Study instruction:
- Design a military decision-support checklist that requires source validation, uncertainty disclosure, alternative hypotheses and explicit human authorization before consequential action.
Key point:
- Faster analysis does not automatically produce better decisions.
- High-stakes systems must preserve meaningful time for human judgment.
Source links:
- SIPRI: Impact of Military AI on Nuclear Escalation Risk
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
39. Q: What does meaningful human control require in autonomous weapon systems?
A: Show answer
Meaningful human control is not satisfied merely because a person presses a start button or formally approves a system. Human decision-makers need sufficient information, time, training and authority to understand the system’s intended function, anticipate likely effects, constrain its use and intervene or deactivate it when necessary. The level of control must be appropriate to the weapon’s predictability, target type, operational environment and potential harm. The ICRC recommends prohibiting unpredictable systems and those designed or used to apply force against people, while imposing strict limits and supervision on other autonomous weapons. The legal debate continues, but existing international humanitarian law remains relevant to the use of weapons incorporating autonomy.
Study instruction:
- Assess a hypothetical autonomous system against predictability, target limits, duration, geographic scope, supervision, intervention and accountability.
Key point:
- Human involvement must be effective, not merely nominal.
- Autonomy does not remove legal and ethical responsibility from people and states.
Source links:
- ICRC: Position on Autonomous Weapon Systems
- United Nations: Autonomous Weapon Systems — Frequently Asked Questions
40. Q: Which international humanitarian law principles apply to cyber operations during armed conflict?
A: Show answer
International humanitarian law applies to cyber operations when they are conducted in the context of an armed conflict and fall within the law’s scope. Core principles include distinction between military objectives and civilians or civilian objects, proportionality in anticipated incidental civilian harm, and precautions to spare civilians. Cyber operations directed against hospitals, civilian infrastructure or public services may raise serious humanitarian concerns. Some legal questions remain contested, including how particular forms of data damage or connectivity disruption should be classified. The important point is that cyberspace is not a law-free domain; the applicable analysis depends on the facts, the armed-conflict context and the effects of the operation.
Study instruction:
- Apply distinction, proportionality and precautions to a hypothetical cyber operation affecting a hospital network or public utility.
Key point:
- Existing humanitarian law applies to cyber operations in armed conflict.
- Civilian systems and essential services require particular protection.
Source links:
- ICRC: International Humanitarian Law Limits the Conduct of Cyber Operations
- ICRC: Cyber Operations and Harmful Information
41. Q: How can international cyber norms and confidence-building measures reduce escalation?
A: Show answer
States can reduce risk by reaffirming that international law applies in cyberspace, committing to protect critical infrastructure, cooperating on incident response and sharing information about threats. Confidence-building measures can include points of contact, communication channels, notification procedures, exercises, transparency about doctrine and cooperation between national computer emergency response teams. These measures do not eliminate malicious activity or resolve every dispute over attribution. Their purpose is to reduce misunderstanding, improve resilience and create ways to manage incidents before political pressure turns them into military confrontation. Norms are most useful when they are supported by domestic implementation, technical capacity and regular review.
Study instruction:
- Draft a bilateral cyber incident protocol covering notification, evidence preservation, technical contact points, public statements and escalation management.
Key point:
- Shared procedures can reduce uncertainty even when strategic rivalry remains.
- Norms need practical implementation and sustained communication.
Source links:
- United Nations: Report on Responsible State Behaviour in Cyberspace (A/76/135)
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
42. Q: Why does the chapter argue that global cooperation does not require nations to become culturally or politically identical?
A: Show answer
Cooperation requires enough shared rules and communication to coordinate action, not the elimination of national identities or local traditions. States can retain different political systems, languages, religions and policy priorities while agreeing on procedures for managing cross-border risks. The chapter’s sports analogy illustrates this distinction: teams can compete fiercely because they accept common rules. In international affairs, shared standards can make disagreement more predictable and reduce the cost of cooperation. The challenge is to negotiate rules that are sufficiently inclusive and legitimate, rather than imposing one society’s values or interests as universal without consultation.
Study instruction:
- Identify a global problem and distinguish the minimum common rules needed to manage it from domestic choices that can legitimately remain diverse.
Key point:
- Cooperation is compatible with pluralism and national independence.
- Common rules organize competition; they need not erase differences.
Source links:
- United Nations Charter: Purposes and Principles
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
43. Q: How can patriotism and global cooperation reinforce rather than contradict each other?
A: Show answer
Patriotism can mean protecting the well-being, security and dignity of one’s community. Many threats to those interests—pandemics, climate change, cyber disruption, financial instability and unsafe AI—cannot be managed by one country alone. Cooperation with foreign governments, researchers and institutions can therefore be a practical way to protect national populations. The distinction is between cooperation that serves legitimate shared interests and arrangements that surrender accountability or impose unfair costs. A government can defend its citizens while supporting international rules, scientific exchange and reciprocal assistance. The chapter’s argument is that national loyalty need not require hostility toward outsiders.
Study instruction:
- For a national policy issue, explain how international cooperation could protect domestic interests and identify the safeguards needed to preserve democratic accountability.
Key point:
- Global cooperation can be an instrument of responsible patriotism.
- National interest and shared human security often overlap.
Source links:
- WHO: Sharing International Health Regulations Information
- United Nations Charter: Purposes and Principles
44. Q: What are the two practical principles of global cooperation emphasized by the chapter?
A: Show answer
First, countries need shared rules for managing relations and competition. Such rules can establish common procedures, safety requirements, reporting duties and limits on conduct while allowing national differences to remain. Second, states sometimes need to give priority to long-term human interests over short-term gains for a few actors. In AI governance, this could mean agreeing not to deploy certain dangerous capabilities even if unilateral deployment appears advantageous. These principles do not imply that every technology should be banned or that governments will stop competing. They establish a framework in which competition is bounded by common safeguards and reciprocal commitments.
Study instruction:
- Draft two principles for a proposed international AI agreement: one governing fair competition and one protecting a long-term shared interest.
Key point:
- Global rules make competition more predictable.
- Long-term shared security can justify limits on short-term advantage.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- OECD: AI Principles
45. Q: Why may AI arms control be harder than regulating nuclear weapons?
A: Show answer
AI capabilities can be developed in smaller facilities, embedded in ordinary software and hardware, distributed through cloud services and adapted for many civilian or military purposes. The same model or component may support logistics, medicine, surveillance, targeting or cyber operations. This makes it difficult to distinguish prohibited military activity from legitimate research and commercial development. Software can also be updated rapidly and transferred across borders, while expertise and computing resources are widely distributed. These characteristics complicate verification, but they do not make agreements impossible. Rules can focus on uses, performance limits, risk controls, human authority, transparency and prohibited applications rather than attempting to control every general-purpose algorithm.
Study instruction:
- Compare nuclear and AI arms-control challenges across detectability, dual use, material signatures, verification, private-sector involvement and speed of change.
Key point:
- AI’s dual-use and software-based nature complicates traditional arms control.
- Use-based rules and lifecycle safeguards may complement capability limits.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- ICRC: Position on Autonomous Weapon Systems
46. Q: How can states verify compliance with AI agreements when civilian and military uses overlap?
A: Show answer
Verification can combine several methods rather than relying on one inspection regime. These may include declarations of relevant systems and uses, independent testing, documentation of model capabilities, controlled access to high-risk deployments, audit trails, incident reporting, procurement rules and review of human-control arrangements. Technical assurance may examine whether systems remain within declared operating limits, while institutional assurance examines who can authorize, monitor and stop deployment. Verification must protect legitimate commercial secrets and privacy without allowing those interests to conceal prohibited activity. Because AI evolves quickly, agreements should include periodic reassessment, dispute resolution and procedures for updating technical definitions.
Study instruction:
- Design a verification package for a hypothetical autonomous weapons agreement and explain what each measure can prove, what it cannot prove and how abuse of the verification process could be prevented.
Key point:
- Verification should combine technical evidence with governance and accountability records.
- No single audit method can resolve every dual-use problem.
Source links:
- ICRC: Position on Autonomous Weapon Systems
- NIST: AI Risk Management Framework
47. Q: What would an effective international AI governance framework need to include?
A: Show answer
It would need shared principles, risk-based obligations, clear institutional responsibilities, meaningful participation by affected communities and practical ways to assess implementation. Core elements include human rights protection, transparency, accountability, safety and security testing, human oversight, incident reporting, remedies for affected people and cross-border cooperation. Different systems can remain legally distinct if they achieve compatible outcomes and recognize common safeguards. The United Nations’ Global Digital Compact provides a framework for inclusive digital cooperation and international AI governance, while UNESCO and the OECD provide complementary ethical and policy principles. Such frameworks are not self-executing: they require national laws, budgets, regulators, technical expertise and regular review.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate an AI governance proposal against rights, risk management, oversight, participation, accountability, interoperability and implementation capacity.
Key point:
- Global principles need institutions and domestic implementation to become effective.
- Interoperability can support cooperation without requiring identical regulatory systems.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
48. Q: Why must developing countries have a meaningful role in global AI governance?
A: Show answer
Rules designed mainly by technologically advanced states may not reflect the infrastructure constraints, languages, development priorities and institutional capacities of lower-income countries. Developing countries are not merely recipients of technology: they contribute data, labor, markets, research, cultural knowledge and public-sector use cases. Meaningful participation can improve legitimacy and ensure that governance addresses capacity-building, affordable compute, local-language systems, data rights and fair value distribution. The Global Digital Compact explicitly calls for inclusive participation and support for developing-country AI capabilities. Representation should involve governments as well as researchers, businesses, civil society and communities affected by AI deployment.
Study instruction:
- Review an international AI policy proposal and identify whose interests are represented, whose are missing and what capacity-building measures would enable meaningful participation.
Key point:
- Inclusive governance is both a fairness requirement and a condition for workable global rules.
- Capacity-building should accompany participation, not be treated as an afterthought.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
49. Q: What is the realist view of international relations discussed in the chapter?
A: Show answer
Realist theories emphasize that the international system lacks a central authority capable of reliably protecting states from one another. States therefore pay close attention to survival, relative power, security competition and the possibility that rivals may exploit vulnerability. This perspective helps explain arms races, alliance formation, mistrust and strategic balancing. It is not a single uniform theory, and realism does not mean that cooperation never occurs. Some realist accounts treat cooperation as limited or conditional because states worry about relative gains and future intentions. The chapter challenges the idea that power competition is an unchangeable law, arguing that institutions, norms and economic conditions can alter state behavior.
Study instruction:
