- Apply a realist explanation to one AI competition scenario, then identify which assumptions about uncertainty, relative gains and security drive the prediction.
Key point:
- Realism highlights insecurity and competition in an anarchic international system.
- It is an analytical framework, not proof that conflict is inevitable.
Source links:
- Mearsheimer: The False Promise of International Institutions
- United Nations Charter: Purposes and Principles
50. Q: What is “veneer theory,” and how does the chapter challenge it?
A: Show answer
Veneer theory is the idea that humans are fundamentally selfish and aggressive, while cooperation and morality are only a thin cultural covering over an unchanging competitive nature. The chapter challenges this view by pointing to cooperation in biological systems and to the historical construction of increasingly large human communities. Research on primates documents prosocial behavior, reconciliation and cooperation alongside competition and aggression. This evidence does not prove that humans are naturally peaceful or that institutions can eliminate conflict. It shows that human behavior includes multiple tendencies and that social arrangements can strengthen or weaken cooperation. The chapter uses this argument to reject the claim that international rivalry is an unavoidable law of nature.
Study instruction:
- Find one example of cooperation and one of competition in animal or human societies, then explain the conditions that support each behavior.
Key point:
- Cooperation and competition coexist in biological and human systems.
- Institutions can shape which tendencies dominate in particular settings.
Source links:
- de Waal and Suchak: Prosocial Primates
- de Waal: The Integration of Dominance and Social Bonding in Primates
51. Q: What does the chapter suggest about the historical growth of human cooperation?
A: Show answer
Harari argues that humans have repeatedly expanded the scale at which strangers can coordinate, moving from small bands to tribes, religions, trade networks, states and global institutions. These larger communities are not natural facts; they depend on shared stories, rules, communication systems, trust and enforcement mechanisms. Cooperation does not remove conflict, and institutions can be unequal or coercive. Nevertheless, the ability to build networks across differences is a major source of human collective power. The chapter’s global-network argument is that digital technology can either extend this capacity through communication or undermine it by separating people into incompatible information environments.
Study instruction:
- Trace one historical institution that enabled cooperation among strangers and identify the shared rules, information channels and enforcement mechanisms that sustained it.
Key point:
- Large-scale cooperation is institutionally constructed and can change.
- Communication and shared procedures make cooperation across difference possible.
Source links:
- United Nations Charter: Purposes and Principles
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
52. Q: What factors does the chapter associate with the decline of large-scale interstate war after 1945?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several interacting factors: nuclear weapons raised the potential cost of great-power war; knowledge-based industries made some forms of conquest less economically rewarding; international institutions and interdependence created channels for cooperation; and cultural attitudes toward militarism changed in many societies. These are explanations to evaluate rather than a complete consensus account. The apparent decline of some forms of war depends on the period, conflict definition and measure used; civil wars, proxy conflicts and organized violence continued, and the trend has not been uniform. The UCDP datasets allow researchers to distinguish interstate, intrastate and other forms of organized violence instead of treating “war” as one undifferentiated category.
Study instruction:
- Use a conflict dataset to compare interstate and intrastate conflict trends since 1946, and state which metric supports each conclusion.
Key point:
- The post-1945 pattern reflects multiple technological, economic, political and cultural factors.
- Claims about a “decline of war” depend on definitions and measurement choices.
Source links:
- Uppsala Conflict Data Program: Dataset Download Center
- PRIO: Armed Conflict 1946–2001 — A New Dataset
53. Q: Why does the chapter insist that the decline of war is reversible, and what do recent military-spending figures show?
A: Show answer
If lower levels of interstate war resulted partly from human decisions, institutions and incentives, then those arrangements can weaken or change. Political leaders may revive militaristic narratives, security dilemmas may intensify and technological competition may encourage rearmament. Current military spending illustrates the point: SIPRI reports that world military expenditure reached $2.887 trillion in 2025, an 11th consecutive year of growth, although the annual increase slowed to 2.9 percent in real terms. Spending is not the same as war, and higher budgets do not prove that conflict is inevitable. They do indicate that security priorities and resource allocations are changing and deserve scrutiny.
Study instruction:
- Compare military expenditure with conflict incidence and explain why spending trends are indicators of strategic priorities, not direct measures of war probability.
Key point:
- Peaceful periods are politically maintained, not guaranteed by history.
- Rising military expenditure signals changing priorities but does not alone establish future conflict.
Source links:
- SIPRI: Trends in World Military Expenditure, 2025
- Uppsala Conflict Data Program: Dataset Download Center
54. Q: How can leaders’ historical narratives become self-fulfilling prophecies in international politics?
A: Show answer
Leaders interpret current threats through beliefs about the past, national identity and the intentions of other states. If they assume that conflict is permanent and that rivals are inherently hostile, they may adopt policies that make compromise harder and encourage others to prepare for confrontation. Those responses can then appear to confirm the original belief. Historical narratives are therefore not merely descriptions; they can shape decisions, budgets, alliances and military action. The remedy is not to ignore history but to test claims against credible evidence, distinguish interpretation from fact, consult independent scholarship and consider how alternative policies might change future incentives.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a political speech or policy document and separate verifiable historical claims, interpretation, threat assumptions and the actions those assumptions justify.
Key point:
- Historical beliefs can influence and intensify present conflict.
- Critical historical analysis can interrupt self-reinforcing threat narratives.
Source links:
- United Nations Charter: Purposes and Principles
- Mearsheimer: The False Promise of International Institutions
55. Q: What should a country do if essential public services become dependent on foreign AI and cloud infrastructure?
A: Show answer
It should first map the services, data, suppliers, subcontractors, legal jurisdictions and technical dependencies involved. It should then assess concentration risk, access controls, continuity arrangements, data portability, audit rights, incident response, exit costs and the availability of alternative providers. Full technological self-sufficiency may be prohibitively expensive and is not always necessary. A more practical objective is strategic resilience: retain control over critical decisions and sensitive data, develop local expertise, diversify where justified, negotiate enforceable service and security obligations, and maintain tested fallback arrangements. Procurement decisions should balance security, cost, performance, competition and the public interest.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a dependency register for one public service and propose mitigation measures for supplier failure, foreign legal access, cyber incident and sudden loss of service.
Key point:
- Digital sovereignty is best assessed through control, resilience and realistic alternatives.
- Diversification and accountable procurement can reduce dependency without requiring total autarky.
Source links:
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
56. Q: How could a developing country reduce the risk of becoming a data colony while still benefiting from global AI services?
A: Show answer
It can combine access to international technology with policies that build local capacity and improve bargaining power. Priorities may include reliable connectivity, affordable compute, representative local-language datasets, data-protection rules, competition policy, research institutions, workforce development and support for domestic firms. Public procurement can require portability, security, auditability and clear rights over data and outputs. Regional cooperation can pool resources that would be costly for one country to build alone. The goal is not to prohibit foreign providers but to ensure that local people and institutions participate in value creation, retain meaningful choices and can govern sensitive uses.
Study instruction:
- Develop a three-year national AI capacity plan using the four foundations of connectivity, compute, context and competency, with indicators for local value creation and accountability.
Key point:
- Participation in the AI economy requires more than access to imported applications.
- Local capability and fair data governance improve bargaining power and resilience.
Source links:
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
- UNCTAD: Digital Economy Report 2021
57. Q: How should governments respond when a cyber incident occurs during a tense military confrontation?
A: Show answer
They should avoid treating the first technical indication as conclusive proof of state responsibility or hostile intent. Initial priorities include protecting essential services, preserving evidence, isolating affected systems where appropriate, coordinating technical responders and assessing civilian impacts. Political and military leaders should receive confidence-rated assessments, alternative explanations and clear distinctions between espionage, disruption and operations that may constitute an armed attack. Communication channels with the suspected state and trusted intermediaries can help prevent misinterpretation. Any response should be lawful, proportionate and based on sufficiently reliable evidence, with particular care to avoid actions that could threaten nuclear command systems or civilian infrastructure.
Study instruction:
- Run a tabletop exercise with technical, diplomatic, legal and military roles; record which decisions require corroboration and which actions can be taken immediately to protect civilians.
Key point:
- Crisis response must combine technical containment with disciplined political communication.
- Premature attribution and disproportionate retaliation can widen a cyber incident into armed conflict.
Source links:
- United Nations: Report on Responsible State Behaviour in Cyberspace (A/76/135)
- ICRC: International Humanitarian Law Limits the Conduct of Cyber Operations
58. Q: How should countries cooperate when AI tools are implicated in a cross-border public-health emergency?
A: Show answer
Countries should share timely and reliable information about the health event, coordinate with designated public-health authorities, communicate uncertainty and avoid concealing relevant evidence for short-term political advantage. AI may assist with analysis, translation, logistics or surveillance, but its outputs need scientific validation and accountable human oversight. Governments should protect privacy, guard against discriminatory use and distinguish verified findings from model-generated hypotheses. The International Health Regulations provide mechanisms for notification and information sharing about events that may cross borders. Effective cooperation also requires trusted scientific institutions, accessible public communication and equitable access to countermeasures.
Study instruction:
- Create an emergency information-sharing workflow showing what data can be shared, by whom, under which safeguards and how uncertain AI-generated assessments are validated.
Key point:
- Timely information exchange is essential for managing transboundary health risks.
- AI can support response but cannot replace public-health judgment and accountability.
Source links:
- WHO: Sharing International Health Regulations Information
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
59. Q: How can policymakers measure whether global digital cooperation is working?
A: Show answer
Measurement should cover both institutional commitments and real-world outcomes. Useful indicators include cross-border incident notification, participation by developing countries, interoperability of governance frameworks, access to connectivity and compute, local-language representation, availability of remedies, transparency of high-risk deployments, protection of critical infrastructure and distribution of economic value. Process indicators alone can be misleading: signing a declaration does not prove implementation, and the number of AI strategies does not show whether rights or safety improved. Evaluations should use baseline data, independent review, disaggregated outcomes and periodic public reporting. Measures should also detect unintended consequences such as excessive fragmentation, market concentration or exclusion of vulnerable groups.
Study instruction:
- Build a balanced scorecard with at least one indicator for safety, rights, inclusion, resilience, interoperability and fair value distribution.
Key point:
- Effective cooperation must be assessed through implementation and outcomes, not declarations alone.
- Metrics should reveal who benefits, who bears risk and who remains excluded.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- World Bank: Digital Progress and Trends Report 2025
60. Q: What is the chapter’s overall lesson about the future of AI and international order?
A: Show answer
The chapter rejects the idea that humanity is condemned either to a new imperial order or to permanent conflict between digital blocs. AI can centralize information and power, intensify economic inequality, create new military risks and divide societies into incompatible information environments. Yet these outcomes are not laws of nature. States can preserve national diversity while agreeing on rules, protect human judgment in high-stakes systems, distribute technological capacity more fairly and maintain channels for cooperation across rival networks. The chapter’s closing argument is that history remains open: because institutions and political orders are human creations, people retain responsibility for shaping them. This is not a guarantee of success; it is a reason to act before dangerous incentives become entrenched.
Study instruction:
- Write a one-page policy synthesis identifying the three greatest risks, three feasible cooperation measures and the political choices needed to implement them.
Key point:
- AI’s geopolitical future is shaped by institutions and choices, not technology alone.
- Cooperation, accountability and pluralism can help prevent digital rivalry from becoming global catastrophe.
Source links:
- United Nations: Global Digital Compact
- OECD: AI Principles
- United Nations Charter: Purposes and Principles
